Perhaps occasionally, while glancing at a mirror in a bathroom or a car, we might pause and experience an intimation of it. The glance turns into a stare and we wonder, "What is that? Am I really inside there?" made of and contained by this body, a brain lodged somewhere in there asking these questions. How did I end up here? Why and how am I a human? In the words of anthropologist and writer Ernest Becker, we see in plain sight that we are in a heartp pumping, breathasping body, a material fleshy casing that is alien. If we look even closer, perhaps putting our nose almost right up against the reflecting surface, we might suddenly come to the visceral realization that our skin is wrapped around us. The hair is always slowly growing out, the flakes of skin always peeling and falling away, the oil always forming and rearranging. Our eyeballs move around in our skull, observing our reflection as it appears back in their small black pupils, which seem to consume reality like black holes sucking everything up into the singularity of consciousness. Regardless of how frequent or explicit one might experience this kind of moment, arguably all of us do experience a similar kind of phenomenon on a regular basis, just with less conscious intensity and explicitness. Every time we interact with or perceive others, there is at least a faint recognition and awareness that others can or are perceiving us. Depending on who we are, this recognition and awareness might be far more than faint. Regardless, in all cases, a feedback loop of mutual perception and objectification ensues. We perceive and judge each other from the outside, mapping assumptions onto behaviors and behaviors back onto assumptions. Other people become like mirrors. We see our reflection in their gaze and we feel the existential weight of being alive and aware. Describing the way our sense of self emerges through social reflection, sociologist Charles Kulie coined the term the looking glass self, which he defined as a three-fold process. First, we imagine how we appear to others. Second, we imagine how others judge our appearance. And third, we experience some sort of pride or shame associated with these imagined judgments and form our self-concept partly based on these feelings over time. Kulie writes, "As we see our face, figure, and dress in the glass and are interested in them because they are ours, so in imagination, we perceive in another's mind some thought of our appearance, manner, aims, deeds, character, friends, and so on, and are variously affected by it." This also echoes a core concept in the work of 20th century existentialist philosopher Jean Paul Sartra who argued that our sense of self is significantly shaped by imagining the perspectives that others have of us. Our self-consciousness is revealed, exposed and often distorted through the gaze of others, perhaps creating more of an existential prism than a mirror. And in our attempt to interpret and align with how others see us, we often modify ourselves, including our own self-perception. This was famously explored and expressed in Sartra's play, No Exit, where three characters who have just died find themselves confined inside a small room with no windows or mirrors. At first, they don't fully understand where they are or what's happening. Soon, however, with each person now limited to knowing their appearance and character, only through how the others describe and judge them, it becomes clear they are in hell being tortured by each other. Estelle, one of the three characters, laments at one point. But how can I rely upon your taste? Is it the same as my taste? Oh, how sickening it all is enough to drive one crazy. The torture is their self-nowledge being constrained and affected by the others whose validation and control always seems to elude them. This is where Sartra's famous line comes from. Hell is other people. Arguably, this is why it can often feel so strange or exhausting to be seen by others. to interact with others. We feel a dependency on their perceptions to understand and form our own. But in this dependency, we are compelled to be hypervigilant over how we come off. And if we want to ensure we come off in a way that is favorable, authentic, or both, we must constantly know, oversee, or modify ourselves. It is as if we are both the artist and security guard in an art gallery that solely contains the sculpture of our being. Everyone that walks by wants it to look a certain way from where they stand. If we fail in our role or feel as though we have, it's not because we aren't good at our job. It's because it's an impossible job to always do well. In the words of psychoanalyst Jacqu Lon, I see only from one point, but in my existence, I am looked at from all sides. Whether it's how we change the way we walk or talk when we encounter strangers. Whether it's how we dress or present our hair and makeup differently around certain people, or whether it's how and why we make decisions in life based on the expectations of those around us. We all experience the often hellish nature of being perceived. But in truth, hell isn't other people. It's us. It's how much we depend on other people seeing us certain ways. It's how much we depend on our reflection coming back to us as beautiful, pristine, controllable, and as expected. Arguably, the root of this dependency and subsequent disqu is the same one that causes the strange, disorienting experience of staring at ourselves closely in a mirror or becoming hyper aware of our body. We suddenly expand the scope of our awareness beyond the surface of our normal unmediated state, burrowing into our being. But ultimately we don't really know or understand what we find in the sense of what we think we are looking for. A solid, clear, consistent, authentic self. We find nothing. Everything dissolves away and only the strange arbitrariness of our existence remains. Often in these moments, we are reminded that our body is something other in constant motion largely beneath our awareness. We are reminded that for the most part, we played no role in forming it or the material that comprises it. We have merely appeared here in this place, in this body, in this mind, in this self. All of which, like the body, are also in constant motion and largely beneath our awareness and control. In our eyes, we see the windows to a vehicle we are fundamentally passengers to. When we loathe or resist this condition, fear or fret. When we yearn for control and to know ourselves in the world in a unified, static, consistent form, when we depend on things and people to always align with our expectations and desires, we stay in hell. Not because we don't find an ideal stable self or world, but because we expect and depend on what can never be had. If instead of fretting over or fighting it, we see and know the flux and absurdity and contingency of all things and we accept and embrace it, then we see that the doors of hell are always open. They are inside our mind and we control and guard them. We can at least on occasion walk out and see beyond the confines of the small room of judgment and fear and ego and hypervigilance. Outside the doors, there's nothing to resolve, nothing to fight, nothing to understand. There's only something to marvel at and wonder about like a 2-year-old child recognizing the reflection for the first time. And if you ever find yourself worried, hell is always there. If this video resonated with you, my new book explores many of the same ideas. Broadly, game theory is the mathematical study of decision-m and strategies in situations where outcomes depend on others choices. More specifically, it examines the nature and effects of how conflict and cooperation among rational decision makers can lead to optimal or suboptimal payoffs. With all this in mind, what is the most effective decision-making strategy or approach in temperament and life in general? Is there one? In 1980, political scientist Robert Axelrod set out to test and answer this very question. Using computer programs to model different decision-making strategies, Axelrod orchestrated an experiment. He had leading theorists from various disciplines and places around the world create and submit programs that would compete in a tournament of an iterated version of the prisoners dilemma. The goal was to submit the best strategy and win. The rules of the tournament were simple. Each player or program played a single game against every other player as well as a copy of itself. In each game, each player had the option to either cooperate with or defect against their opponent. If both players cooperated, they both received three points. If one cooperated and the other defected, the player who defected received five points and the player who cooperated received zero. If both defected, both received one point. There is an algorithm on a website right now that through a fixed and predetermined set of parameters contains every sentence and image that has ever and could ever be written and seen. In 1941, the Argentine short story writer and essaist Jorge Luis Bourhees published a short story titled The Library of Babel. In the story, there is a seemingly infinite library with hexagonal floors going up and down without any apparent end. On each floor, there are shelves of books. Inside all the books appears to be gibberish, random letters and punctuations. The characters in the story initially have no idea what the books are or what they mean. To them, they mean nothing other than perhaps confusion, intrigue, or wasted space. Eventually, however, the characters come to a realization. The books contain every possible combination of the letters of the alphabet, a space, a comma, and a period. Amidst all the gibberish is also every complete comprehensible work that has ever and could ever be written. At first the characters are overjoyed. The narrator of the story says all men felt themselves the possessors of an intact and secret treasure. There was no personal problem, no world problem whose eloquent solution did not exist somewhere in some hexagon. The universe was justified. The universe suddenly became congruent with the unlimited width and breadth of humankind's hope. Quickly, however, this joy turns to madness and despair as the discovery amounts to very little. The characters soon realize they will likely never find anything of meaning. The narrator continues, "The certainty that some bookshelf and some hexagon contain precious books, yet that those precious books were forever out of reach was almost unbearable. The library is simply too vast and meaning too sparse. And so, it becomes a prison of certainty, forever guarded by futility." Bourhees's short story is essentially a literary expression of a thought experiment in mathematical probability that would come to be known as the infinite monkey theorem. Essentially, the thought experiment goes as follows. If a monkey were given a typewriter and an infinite amount of time, hitting keys independently and at random, it would almost certainly produce any and every possible text from the Bible to the Odyssey to the complete works of Shakespeare. From a philosophical point of view, this mirrors a modal principle where over an infinite time scale, anything with a nonzero probability of occurring will occur with certainty. There are, of course, numerous critiques of the infinite monkey theorem. Most critiques, however, seem to miss the point. Firstly, it's a thought experiment. Secondly, it's a thought experiment designed to demonstrate the probability and concept of infinity, not a way of making practical predictions. Trying to map out intuitions or smaller scale assessments and predictions onto an infinite scale is a failing mission from the start. But of course, that isn't to say people haven't tried. In 2003 at Payton Zoo in England, six Makox were given a computer with the keyboard. The aim was to replicate a small scale version of the infinite monkey theorem and see what they would produce. After about a month, the Makox generated five pages in total. Each page was mostly filled with the letter S. Then the Makox destroyed the computer. In that same year, with the onset of more powerful software programs, the thought experiment was also attempted with virtual monkeys through an independent web project known as the monkey Shakespeare simulator. Running a program designed to simulate the same sort of random typing of real monkeys. The experiment generated a partial line from Shakespeare's Henry IV part two. In 2004, a separate program developed by Dan Oliver generated a 19 character sequence from Shakespeare's The Two Gentlemen of Arona. Estimation suggested that this sequence would have taken somewhere around 4.2 * 10 28th power in years or 42 bill162,500,000 billion billion years to accomplish in reality. What these experiments seem to actually reveal is that infinity cannot be tested or experimented in a finite system with finite constraints. Nor can it be fathomemed by the human mind. This is important because it helps us make a little bit better sense of the absurdity of what was alluded to earlier. The real world version of Bourhees's Library of Babel. An unlikely, unprecedented, self-aware, symbolic, toolmaking, existence questioning species that nothing could have ever predicted before us, that changed everything, and whose existence is now completely normal and obvious. Only because of the apparent chaos and randomness of the cosmos do we exist. And as beings forged by the very fire of chaos, we can also learn to better handle and withstand the flames. Another concept spearheaded by Taleb is known as anti-fragility. This idea refers to things and systems that benefit and grow stronger through exposure to disorder and randomness. This includes things like evolution, the immune system, decentralized free markets, airlines, the internet, and knowledge itself. As these things are exposed to stressors or errors, they adapt and strengthen, growing more capable of thriving under uncertainty in the future. And of course, this also includes us. If we are willing to take and face risks, undergo potential harm, and move forward through an uncertain existence with mental fortitude and resilience, doing our best to prepare for what we think will occur while accepting the events that actually do, growing from them as they shape the course of history with and through us, then we too can be antifragile. Ultimately, we don't know what black swans might be lurking around us right now, grazing in the shadows of unknowability, floating in the mires of uncertainty. We are always at the edge, where light meets shadow, where stable land meets boggy ground, projecting out in all directions are comfortable narratives of linearity and predictability. But from the surface of our expectations all the way down into our most core beliefs, so much of what we expect and hold to be true could reveal at any point suddenly and all at once to be deeply flawed, incomplete, or flatout wrong. The laws of nature, our position in the cosmos, the very condition of the cosmos itself, the realities of consciousness and selfhood, our idea of what's right and wrong, what's important and what our purpose is, our expectations of the future. We don't know what is true and we don't know what will happen, but we don't need to. At bottom, we don't even want to. The apparent randomness and unpredictability, as much as our minds work to smooth it over and fill in the cracks, provide the slants, slopes, and gaps that thrill us, that build us. If we wish to improve our experience in a chaotic universe, perhaps instead of always trying to predict or control events, worrying about what might happen or has happened, we should learn to enjoy the bird watching from the roller coaster ride that is existence. Something that very few people could have predicted just a couple decades ago is the economy built on personal information. We are not defined by what we do, but by what we don't do. Everything we choose to not participate in, to not have, to not strive for. Everyone we choose to not spend time with, to not associate with, chisels away at the cement block of possibility and reveals the sculpture of our being. We are so often told that success is an additive process that we gain it by doing more, having more, and being more. But this is after a certain point backwards. Success is largely subtractive. We gain it by removing and blocking more from our life. To be clear, success here doesn't refer strictly to societal or financial achievements. Rather, it refers to having the most preferable experience of the greatest amount of one's time, more synonymous with a sort of satisfaction or happiness in and about one's life. This might include societal and financial achievements, but it does not necessitate them at any specific scale. Of course, what is the most preferable experience of one's time can only be determined by each individual for themselves. But that is part of the problem. For so many of us, we have a certain vision of what success is and what is preferable. Not because we truly believe in it, not because we've reflected deeply on it and tested against it, but because it's what we've been instructed and what we've assumed is true. For many of us, we've viewed life from a perspective of lack and assume success is always an additive process. It is as if we've been sifting for gold with a mesh too fine to let anything pass through. We hold on to everything believing it's all valuable and good. But in the end, we are left with a pan full of sand and dirt. To return any balance and regain any control over our life to find the gold, we must flip the whole thing over again and use a filter we've thoughtfully selected. One that lets the sand and dirt pass through. Instead of pursuing more people, more pleasures, more activities, more possessions, and in some cases, more achievements, we can often find greater satisfaction in life by having fewer of these things. The great pessimist philosopher Arthur Schopenhau argued that happiness is best achieved not by pursuing pleasure, but by minimizing pain and suffering. He writes, "All satisfaction or what is commonly called happiness is really and essentially negative only. That is, it consists in freedom from pain." If we work to eliminate unnecessary sources of stress, anxiety, and harm, we will likely have a greater positive impact on our state of being than if we work toward adding, let's say, a new car or more impressive social schedule. Author and statistician Nicholas Taleb mirrors the same Schopenhauian idea. The greatest most robust contribution to happiness comes from the elimination or reduction of unhappiness, particularly chronic stress, anxiety, and sources of dissatisfaction. The aim here isn't minimalism or a lifestyle chiefly focused on always living with less. Instead, we might call what we're after here precisionism. An approach to life that aims for clarity and rigor in choices, in yeses and nos, in what one removes and preserves, all for the purposes of reducing unnecessary noise and potential harm in life. Though this may often overlap with minimalism, the key difference is that precisionism doesn't seek less for its own sake. Rather, less is merely often a byproduct of being more precise about what one allows or keeps in their life and why. The term precisionism also appears in art history, referring to a modernist movement in which artists often limit the use of detail, incorporating geometric shapes, clean lines, and stark contrasts of light to create clear focuses and orientations. In these artworks, the limiting of elements, showcasing only what is deemed essential to a subject, does not strip away meaning, but creates it. This is a fitting artistic style to share the term with. We can begin to better determine the essential qualities and details of our own lives by beginning to trust our own sensibilities more. It is often said that when considering options and decisions in life, if it's not a hell yes, it's a hell no. This is a decent start. When it comes to more basic matters like whether to buy a new item of clothing, whether to agree to a meeting, whether to go to a social event, or whether to continue reading a new book, this attitude can certainly be helpful. Perhaps for a slightly more precise approach, we might modify it to be if it's not at least an 8 out of 10, it's a hell no. For more complicated decisions, however, like whether to move, whether to quit or take a job, or whether to start or end a relationship, there are, of course, far more unknown and intricate variables that can rarely be distilled down to a pure hell yes 8 out of 10 reaction. Moreover, things can often be scary and uncomfortable, and we might say hell no simply out of fear or discomfort, even if a yes aligns more with our true goals and hopes. To help better determine larger, more complicated decisions, we might look at how many reasons we have to justify a particular choice. Though perhaps it might seem counterintuitive, it can be argued that the more reasons one uses to justify a choice, the less likely it is to be the right choice. This is because when something requires multiple justifications, it often means there's no single precise reason or that the reasons are vague and insufficient, as excessive rationalization often signals doubt, noise, or selfdeception. For example, one doesn't need more than one reason to not jump into a lion's pen. It would be absurd for one to say that they shouldn't do this because the lion will likely eat them and because it's a bit of a difficult jump and because they're a bit tired today. The less justifications for something, the more likely it is to be the stronger choice. Of course, here too it is not always that simple. Sometimes there are decisions that are simply too complex to reduce the clear singular justifications. For example, choosing a treatment for a condition like cancer may involve multiple serious considerations like survival rate, quality of life, side effects, cost, and long-term risks. Also, sometimes we can easily convince ourselves that a reason is clear out of fear or a cognitive bias. Ultimately, sometimes we will have to make choices blind and accept whatever comes. And sometimes we will make the wrong choices even with clear sight. It is not a perfect system and we are very fallible beings. All we can do is try our best. And we try our best by reconsidering the filter through which our decisions are made, adhering to our personal preferences and considering seriously the subtractive process of success. With all this in mind, we might consider a few specific areas that are potentially worth further sculpting. Less people. How many people do we oblige and stay close with simply because they are there? Of course, there is no universally right number of people to be friends and acquaintances with, but there are certainly wrong people to be friends and acquaintances with, or perhaps, but more accurately, wrong reasons to be friends and acquaintances. Proximity, fear of being alone, and the desire for the approval of others are just some of them. For most of us, beyond a certain relatively low number, relationships can quickly begin to feel more superficial, transactional, or distant. And depending on one's goals and preferences, such relationships may offer little value or meaning. The point here isn't some specific number of people or some frequency of social activity. It's about having clarity on which relationships and interactions we choose to invest in and leaving the rest out. Less commitments. How often do we agree to do things simply because we can, because we have the time in our schedule? Or perhaps we agree because we think it could be a good opportunity or it could be fun and we don't want to let it pass by. Here we might ask ourselves, what is the opportunity really for? Will even the most optimal of outcomes contribute to what we truly want and value in life? And in the case of fun things that we don't want to miss out on, what metric of fun are we using? Is it something society considered fun or something that we are personally interested in or know we enjoy? We should, of course, say yes to both what we can and want to do if we have the choice. But what is feasible is not always reasonable. And what sounds like fun or an opportunity can easily turn into an obligation or chore if it's not aligned with what we truly want. Or if it's stacked on a pile of other opportunities and activities that we never quite have the energy or dedication to enjoy, less stuff. How many times do we buy the newest phone, laptop, console, or whatever else merely because it's the newest one? How many times do we buy an item of clothing or a kitchen gadget because we kind of like it? How often do we make serious financial decisions like going to college or buying a car or a home? Because it's just what you do. Look around in your closet, garage, living room, kitchen, bedroom. How much would really be lost if much of it disappeared? Look around at your life. How much of it do you really need to feel the way you always do? Less stimulation. It has never been easier to numb one's brain with the numbing solution of stimulation. With access to nearly everything, we can readily feel and think nothing, that is, nothing of deep personal merit, meaning, or advantage. How often do we feel the need to stay up on all the latest news, shows, and feeds? How often do we really care about what is contained in most of these things? There's nothing wrong with being engaged, being active, or having a relatively busy schedule. But when stimulation is for its own sake, particularly when it's constant, we have likely entered into a hazardous territory. one where we simply seek to distract ourselves from the fact that we have long since let go of the reigns of our life. Less concern. By chiseling each of these categories and perhaps others unmentioned, what we are often left with, if done right, is less concern. We reduce unnecessary sources of anxiety, overwhelm, stress, and detachment. And we allow space for both the honing in and incorporating of what we find meaningful and valuable. In the words of Henry David Thorough, if one advances confidently in the direction of his dreams and endeavors to live the life which he has imagined, he will meet with a success unexpected in common hours. In proportion, as he simplifies his life, the laws of the universe will appear less complex, and solitude will not be solitude, nor poverty poverty, nor weakness weakness. Of course, it must be said that not everyone can approach life in this way. And simplicity can be ironically quite complex. But if or when we can view success and satisfaction as a process of subtraction, if or when we are able to reconsider what we allow or retain in our life, we should. **Channel:** Pursuit of Wonder | **Views:** 406K | **Date:** 6 months ago | **Duration:** 13:47 | **ID:** 2lAjQZp7ou4

This video is sponsored by the visual learning app, Imprint. Discover life-changing insights from philosophy, psychology, and much more. Get a free 7-day trial and 20% off using my link in the description. The year is 1612. Sasaki Kojito waits on the shore of a small island near Shimosaki, Japan. He stares out at the horizon, a katana in his hand. He waits to prove himself as the greatest swordsman in all of Japan. That or death. By now, the horizon should contain his opponent. Instead, it contains only the endlessness of anticipation and the heat of increasing frustration. After several hours go by, well past the scheduled time of the duel. Finally, a faint silhouette of a boat appears in the distance. On it is Miiamoto Mousashi. The swordsman Kojiro must defeat to secure his legacy. As the boat wades closer, moving up and down with the tide, acquiescent yet steady, Kojito pulses with anger. the pounding of blood echoing in his ears. Once at the shore, Mousashi calmly exits the boat and approaches Kojido. Instead of a katana, he appears to hold a wooden sword, a Bokudo. It's difficult for Kojito to see at first with the setting sun shining brightly behind Mousashi. As Mousashi reaches Kojido with a steady voice, he says, "You have already lost." Enraged by Mousashi's tardiness, his choice of weapon and his arrogance upon arrival. Unable to let another second slip by, Kojito rushes Mousashi. With a lifetime's worth of force, he swings his katana, the fire of rage and ego lighting up his eyes. With an advantaged position with the sun at his back, Mousashi dodges Kojito's slash and returns it with a precise and powerful blow to his head. In an instant, Cojito falls, his body collapsing into the water's edge, bubbles emanating from his mouth, each taking with them emotion and ego from his now lifeless body. Although likely exaggerated, this is the story of a real duel that took place in the 17th century between two of Japan's most respected swordsmen, Sasaki Kojiro and Miiamoto Mousashi. The story is often interpreted as an allegory of two clashing philosophies with Kojido representing orthodox technique and skill and Mousashi representing a methodical unorthodox and cerebral approach. The lateness, the slowness, the way he used the sun, water, weapon, and psychology of the moment were all part of Mousashi's plan. A reflection of his philosophical almost spiritual approach to both combat and life in general. To better understand the philosophy and insights of Mousashi, it is important to understand the story and context of his life. Born in 1584 in Harimma Province, Japan, Mousashi was the son of Shinman Munisai, a renowned martial artist and master swordsman. A brutish and aggressive figure, widely respected and feared. Mousashi, however, was an exception to this respect and fear, often clashing with his father and displaying disobedience and resentfulness. Because of this, at around age seven, Mousashi was disowned by his father. Soon after, he would be taken in by his uncle Durbo, a Buddhist monk practicing at the Shyan Temple near Hiapuku. Here, Mousashi obtained a very different education from the one from his father. Learning from Buddhist teachings and becoming increasingly exposed to various forms of art and literature, Mousashi would begin to absorb and incorporate a spiritual element into his perspective. Lessons of stillness, impermanence, and non-attachment. Soon, a unique and powerful dichotomy began to take shape within Mousashi, one that would go on to define his life and legacy. The disposition for battle and the wisdom of passivity. In the years that followed, Mousashi would exhibit one of history's most impressive feats of combat and martial arts. With a career comprising 60 duels, the first occurring at only age 13, each duel fought against highly skilled, formidable opponents. Many of them fought to the death. One can only wonder how Mousashi's life ended from illness and old age. Despite his unfathomable, albeit likely exaggerated career in combat, arguably Mousashi's most impressive and enduring achievements were not his victories in battle, but rather his victories in philosophy, in understanding the self and the mind. Though perhaps in his case, these two things, philosophy and battle, are one and the same. Before passing around the age of 60, Mousashi produced two written works, The Book of Five Rings and Doodo, or The Way of Walking Alone. The Book of Five Rings is a more practical exploration of Mousashi's philosophy on strategy, particularly as it pertains to combat and martial arts, while DoD expresses a more holistic and almost spiritual perspective on life in general, listing out Mousashi's 21 precepts for living well. Though these two works differ significantly in form and application, both explore the concepts of dedication, strategy, acceptance, detachment, self-reliance, and meaning. All highly influenced by the philosophical and spiritual modes of thought of Zen Buddhism and Daoism. The overarching theme of Mousashi's philosophy can perhaps best be summarized with the concept of focus. He frequently refers to focus not just in the conventional sense of concentration but also in much deeper forms like clear intent, devoted attention, emotional control, perceptiveness in a kind of mental emptiness and adaptability. Fundamentally, no matter who we are and what we do, focus is everything. It is the center of everything we do. Where, when, how, and for how long we direct our attention determines what we feel, how we act and react, what we achieve, and ultimately who we are, and who we become. Throughout his works, Mousashi refers to carrying out well-disiplined focus as the do or the way. Following the way is, in essence, diligently following one's unique path toward mastery, adaptability, and self-reliance. For Mousashi, all of this begins with acceptance. In the first precept of Doodo, he writes, "Acept everything just the way it is." At first, this might sound strange, something so passive being a fundamental precept of a warrior. But for Mousashi, passivity of this form is power. Drawing from ideas in Buddhism, Mousashi suggests that we must never act on illusion. And whether rooted in wishful thinking or emotionally driven preference, our refusal to accept reality creates a foundation of illusion. One that distracts us away from what is true and what can be done. Accept what is and your ability to act and react without hesitation improves. The amount of energy wasted on fighting inevitabilities reduces and once focus centers. Though it can often seem like our preferences determine the quality of our experience in life, it is rather often our ability to shift with the world that regularly opposes our preferences that enhances the quality of our experience. And perhaps more importantly, this plasticity also enhances our effectiveness. In precept 11 of Doodo, Mousashi writes, "In all things have no preferences. By having rigid stringent preferences, the outside world which we cannot control, gains control over us, extending outside our reach as it diverges from our elucory expectations. As a result, we often and easily become distracted and emotionally unsettled. In reducing our preferences and expectations, however, or at least being more supple and agile with them, we shorten the mental bridges we must cross to meet reality. And in doing so, we maintain better equinimity in our mood and our focus. Of course, this is much easier said than done. One cannot simply break free from all preferences or always fully reduce their rigidness. Even wanting no preferences is a preference. But we don't need to break free from all preferences, at least not totally, to still derive the meaning and effectiveness of Mousashi's principle. What matters more, it seems, is what kind of preferences we concern ourselves with, why, and how. Arguably, our most fundamental preference in life is feeling good. For most of us, this is likely an impossible preference to shake free from. But we don't need to. Rather, we can in fact use this foundational preference to help orient our focus. For Mousashi, what is worthy of our attention is in part found through the distinguishment between pleasure and pleasure for its own sake. In precept 2 of Doodo, he writes, "Do not seek pleasure for its own sake." In life, we can feel good as a result of something like the sweetness of a sugary dessert or something consequential like the achievement of a long-held goal. In the case of the dessert, the experience is both the end and the means. In other words, pleasure for its own sake. Whereas the pleasure experienced as a result of an achievement is the byproduct of other acts. The former is momentary, impulsive, and often physical, the latter is often holistic, meaningful, and advantageous. For Mousashi, what we should pursue and focus on here is clear. It's useful to note, however, that Mousashi does not explicitly say that indulgent pleasures like luxury clothing, housing, cuisine, or desserts are inherently bad. Rather, the pursuance and prioritization of them will simply result in a deviation from the way. Ultimately, pleasure for its own sake is a distraction from our focus, whereas other consequential pleasures are often a product of our focus. a more sustainable and effective way of feeling good. With all this in mind, for many of us, we are still left to wonder, what is the way? How do we determine the way for ourselves? Once one has accepted things as they are, reduce the fixedness of their preferences and realize that pleasure is not meaningful in itself, how does one determine what to focus on from here? For Mousashi, this determination can only be made by each individual for themselves. However, crucially, he suggests we must make these determinations through full and clear senses of reason, avoiding impulsiveness, sentimentality, or vague intuition. In precept 3, he writes, "Do not under any circumstances depend on a partial feeling." In other words, we must carefully inspect our thoughts and potential choices for traces of ego, anger, fear, pride, or false preferences, discarding any options that are tainted by these qualities, and instead work toward choices and actions grounded in holistic clarity, reason, and confidence. If we don't feel comprehensive clarity or confidence about the reasons for something, then perhaps it isn't the right thing, and we should keep moving. Of course, this is also much easier said than done. It can often be very hard to tell the difference between emotional decisions and well-reasoned ones. The paths to battles we choose to fight are full of mental booby traps. But perhaps in some cases, we must apply Mousashi's wisdom to the impossibility of ever fully carrying it out. Perhaps sometimes we must accept our inabilities and failures. In the end, even if we arrive on the right battlefield, the fight over our attention will remain constant. The world swings and slashes at it from every angle, including from inside ourselves. But with Mousashi's final words in Doko, never stray from the way. He reminds us to always continue to fight, to endure, to focus, to stay steady. We must each of us try our best to stay true to our hopes and values. No matter what enemy invades us, be it the influences of others, the lower impulses of the body, the distracted nature of our thoughts, or the chaos of all things, we must fight like Mousashi in his duel against Kojiro. Calculated, prepared, aware, patient, confident, adaptable, and willing to strike when the time is right. And when the battle finally comes to an end, hopefully we can at least say, "We gave it everything we had." In the words of Mousashi, when you are fighting a battle to the death, you must employ all the weapons at your disposal. It is deplorable to die with a weapon yet undrawn. The link for it is in the description. Aldis Huxley is perhaps one of the most prophetic and unsettling writers of the 20th century, perhaps of all of modern history. He sits alongside George Orwell in the scope, accuracy, and eeriness of his assessments, predictions, and warnings about what was to potentially come of the modern Western world. How technology, progress, and power would increasingly rub up against freedom, truth, and human meaning. His work poses questions about what it means to be free, to desire truth, and meaning, to be consequential, and connected. and it warns about how easily humans can and will sacrifice these things in the name of comfort, pleasure, and order, often without even realizing it. The American author and educator Neil Postman compared the fears of Huxley to the fears of Orwell. Orwell feared those who would deprive us of information. Huxley feared those who would give us so much that we would be reduced to passivity and egoism. Orwell feared that the truth would be concealed from us. Huxley feared the truth would be drowned in a sea of irrelevance. Orwell feared we would become a captive culture. Huxley feared we would become a trivial culture preoccupied with some equivalent of the felies, the orgy porgy and the centrifugal bumble puppy. In short, Orwell feared that what we hate will ruin us. Huxley feared that what we love will ruin us. Huxley was born in 1894 in Godming, England. As a child, he was immediately exposed to highlevel scientific thinking as his grandfather th Huxley was a famous biologist who worked with Charles Darwin and coined the term agnostic. This would surely be influential on Aldis, likely contributing to his initial interest in science and his later perceptiveness around the implications of scientific progress, particularly the developing overlap of psychology and technology. In adolescence, Huxley initially wanted to be a doctor. However, after contracting an eye disease, he essentially went blind for around 3 years, and his hopes of a career in medicine abruptly ended. Though surely devastating to Huxley at the time, one clearly never knows the bad news from the good news. One bout of illness in one person can change the world forever. Instead of pursuing medicine, Huxley would go on to enroll in Baleo College, Oxford, where he would study English literature and begin writing poetry and short stories. He later described what he experienced as an ordered universe in a world of planless incoherence. The chemical plant was for him a microcosmic representation of the society forming around it. A society increasingly hellbent on perfect order and technological advancement in spite of human experience. A society that focused on mass production and output. A society where human identities began and ended with their roles in the process of set production. A society in which individuals were increasingly produced and managed in the same manner as the assembly lines and technologies that they operated. Huxley's experience at the plant, alongside his developing views of the modern world in general, would ultimately form the foundation for what he would go on to explore in his masterpiece, Brave New World. The novel Brave New World was published in 1932. By this point, Huxley had published several other works of literature and had become increasingly engaged in various intellectual and artistic circles of the time. In the novel, we primarily follow the main characters, Bernard Marx, Lenina Crown, John the Savage, and Helm Holtz Watson as they navigate a future dystopian global society known as the world state. While visiting a reservation outside the world state, Bernard and Lenina encounter a woman who was left behind on a previous trip named Linda and her son Jon, who was born and has lived on the reservation his entire life. Bernard brings Linda and Jon back to London with him, the heart of the world state, essentially despite a high-ranking administrator who had abandoned Linda and was unknowingly and ludely Jon's father, as well as to improve his own social and career standing and ultimately acquire more from society. Once in London, however, Jon soon goes against Bernard's efforts and his focus turns toward understanding and subsequently criticizing and revoling against the world state. His values which he primarily formed from his exposure to Shakespeare and experiences outside the world state cause him to find the society entirely hollow and vapid at best. This divide between him and the state become a key point of contrast used to put forth the brilliant and challenging philosophical questions asked by the novel. In the world state, everything is perfectly ordered, structured, stable, and on the surface very pleasant. Everyone is happy. Citizens are born through an artificial process in a hatchery where embryos are treated differently in order to alter individuals bodies and minds and predetermine their cast and function in society. Everyone is more or less the same in their cast and everyone is conditioned to be content and suitable in their cast. Conflict, dysfunction, and dissatisfaction are thus minimized. Homogeneity is prioritized. In daily life, pleasures and entertainments abound. If anyone isn't happy, they are quickly reminded to take the drug soma, which evokes euphoria and pleasant hallucinations. The world's stable now. People are happy. They get what they want, and they never want what they can't get. And if anything should go wrong, there's Soma, said the world controller, Mustafa Mond to John. In the world state, everything is set up perfectly for continuous stability and constant happiness. It is essentially the perfect outcome of a successful social utopia. And yet, it is deeply unsettling. Freedom, identity, depth, and expression are fundamentally lost. Or rather, they're no longer even relevant or necessary. Individuals only have value in so far as they can provide utility to the societal machine. Love is shallow. Monogamy and family are condemned, obsolete. Everyone belongs to everyone else which citizens repeat as part of their social conditioning. Everyone is not only a bolt on the societal machine but also an output and product of it to be used and consumed. Happiness in the form of pleasure takes precedence over depth and complexity. And so like so many other aspects of life, love and expression and connection are no longer necessary. Why make art? Why love deeply? Why contemplate existence? And why strive for change when there's nothing to struggle over or for? Nothing to uniquely share when you and everyone are finally happy. That's the price we have to pay for stability. You've got to choose between happiness and what people used to call high art. Actual happiness always looks pretty squalid in comparison with the overcompensations for misery. And of course, stability isn't nearly so spectacular as instability. And being contented has none of the glamour of a good fight against misfortune, none of the pictures of a struggle with temptation or a fatal overthrow by passion or doubt. Happiness is never grand, continued Mand to John. At the time of writing Brave New World, Huxley was not extraordinarily concerned about any sort of similar dystopia unfolding onto the real world anytime soon. In fact, the novel was initially written largely as a parody of other popular utopian novels of the time, which Huxley found abhorrent. He wrote, "They evoked in me an almost pathological reaction in the direction of cynical anti- idealism. So much so that before I finished, I had resolved to write a dice of parody of this most optimistic of utopias." Huxley did see the importance of the subject in the implications of what was essentially underpinning such utopian ideas. The sort of idealism that viewed technological and scientific progress as definitively moving toward a perfect enlightened future. He saw the likelihood of this increasingly popular attitude and the modes with which it was being carried out, leading to some form of a brave new world if left unchecked, but just in a more distant future. However, later in his life, he began to change his mind about this. He wrote, "In 1931, when Brave New World was being written, I was convinced that there was still plenty of time. The completely organized society, the scientific cast system, the abolition of free will by methodical conditioning, the servitude made acceptable by regular doses of chemically induced happiness, the orthodoxies drumed in by nightly courses of sleep teaching. These things were coming all right, but not in my time. Not even in the time of my grandchildren. 27 years later, I feel a good deal less optimistic than I did when I was writing Brave New World. The prophecies made in 1931 are coming true much sooner than I thought they would. The nightmare of total organization has emerged from the safe remote future and is now awaiting us just around the next corner. Part of what had drawn concern out of Huxley was the advancing knowledge of psychology in the early to mid 20th century. In particular, the work of the American psychologist BF Skinner and his theory of operant conditioning as well as Ivan Pavlov and his theory of classical conditioning. Fundamentally, operant conditioning is a method of learning and changing behaviors based on rewards and punishments. Part of what Skinner's work notably showcased is that positive reinforcements tend to lead to stronger and longerterm changes in behavior compared to negative reinforcements. This knowledge along with the technologically advancing world that was increasing political access to citizens and citizens to pleasure and entertainment caused Huxley to become increasingly concerned about the kinds of weapons that could and would be used to control, organize, and ostensibly sedate citizens. Unlike the threats of violence and direct force with weapons of conditioning and pleasure, Huxley feared individuals would likely not even recognize that there was a weapon pointed at their heads. worse, they would likely grow to love it, to desire it. He said, "There will be in the next generation or so a pharmacological method of making people love their servitude and producing dictatorship without tears, so to speak, producing a kind of painless concentration camp for entire societies so that people will in fact have their liberties taken away from them, but will rather enjoy it." How accurate Huxley's predictions were for today's and tomorrow's world is up for debate and interpretation. But one cannot deny the fact that at the very least in the 21st century we find some of Huxley's fears, questions, and prophecies emerge at least in a symbolic sense. We might personally find some of his fears in our habits, in our daily lives, in our societal roles, in our desires, in our identities, and what we are and are not aware of. And in a broader sense, we can see the increasingly rampant use and apparent need for various drugs and intoxicants, both prescription and recreational. We can see that with what has gone from radios to TVs to computers to smartphones to whatever is next, the opportunity for simple pleasure and simple escape has only become increasingly available and pervasive. Information and entertainment, often mindless and obscene, flood the streets, destroying previously built structures of alleged truth and clarity. What is left behind is a sort of complacency, annihilism, avoid of meaning. And with continued flooding appearing imminent, rebuilding seems almost futile. And so what do we do? We jump in the floodwaters, floating on the surface and our inflatable tubes, cocktails of substance in one hand and a phone or whatever else in the other. We don't become happy about the destruction. We forget there is any. Perhaps slightly deviating from Huxley's views and Brave New World, the cause and process by which these qualities of society have come to be is not simple, obvious, or easily pinpointed onto any sort of isolated groups, rulers, or goals. Is the state of essentially the entire modern world more likely to be the result of the intentional and perfectly executed psychological direction by the few, or the result of the increasingly unrestrained and capitalistically facilitated impulses of the many? or at least a proportional combination of both. Perhaps it is merely convenient for rulers and societies how life and psychology work on their own. Perhaps it is all the more convenient to occasionally know how to tap into it, even if only seldomly with success. BF Skinner famously used rats in his early research on operant conditioning. He put hungry rats in a box that contained a lever on the side. And as the rats moved around the box and accidentally knocked the lever, a food pellet would drop into a container. Soon the rats would begin to intentionally hit the lever over and over. The experiment clearly demonstrates how behavior can be changed through reinforcement and in this case manipulated by intention. But the natural world, the box in which we have all been dropped in, is not an isolated experiment. There is no fundamental or first cause individual or group that is running the show other than perhaps the very substrate force of evolution. We are all compelled, rulers and citizens, by forces outside our control and understanding. Each of us awaken into arbitrary roles as part of the construction and experimentation, hungry and yearning. The only difference is perhaps with humans, we have found ways to make levers and pellets available everywhere, all the time. How could we not all become preoccupied with levers and pellets? There's a saying that if all you have is a hammer, everything looks like a nail. Perhaps likewise, if all you have is desire, everything looks like a lever. Regardless of how we arrived here, regardless of the conditions of the particular time in society we were born into, on an individual level, we all find ourselves with the same basic questions. The same questions Huxley and Brave New World asked. What is one willing to give up for pleasure, for comfort, for happiness, for order, for social cohesion? What is one willing to give up for freedom, for truth, for uniqueness, for art, for love? Would you trade truth for happiness? Would you trade freedom? What is the value of anything if not happiness and feeling good? What makes happiness and goodness real? And why? In Brave New World for John, a world without conflict, a world where mostly everyone is the same, a world of perfect order and unending happiness, is a world that is fundamentally at odds with the nature of being and our truest desires deep down. Desire for independent freethinking, for truth, for identity, for deeper meaning, for art, for love, for struggle. For him, a life worth living is born from the insemination of pleasant and unpleasant experiences constantly knocking against each other. In the words of John, I don't want comfort. I want God. I want poetry. I want real danger. I want freedom. I want goodness. I want sin. In the end, struggle, pain, uncertainty, and sacrifice are unavoidable. It is only what the pain and struggle and sacrifice are for that can be considered, determined, and fought for in each of us. Huxley brilliantly and perceptively reminds us, warns us to consider carefully what we want to sacrifice when the modern world asks us in every moment with increasing fervor. In Huxley's final work, Island, published one year before his passing in 1963, we find an island comprising a more optimistic and hopeful utopia built on honest connection and love and learning and spirituality. We also perhaps find Huxley's last and most potent insight for dealing with the problems of a brave new world shared by the birds on the island. On this island, birds call out a frequent reminder to the inhabitants. Attention. Attention. Here and now, boys. Here and now. The piece seeks to recount real events with real people and real experiences for the purposes of real journalistic insights. But it places the weight of firstirhand experience and imaginative sensibilities equally on the scale of truth. It puts Thompson's imaginative perspective in the story central to it. This literary style would come to be known as Gonzo or Gonzo journalism which was coined and based on Thompson's work originally a prior piece covering the Kentucky Derby. In Gonzo journalism, the endpiece is equal part subjective as it is objective. The journalist is largely central to the story, exaggerating and melding the truth with their imagination and typically humor as opposed to being a detached observer reporting facts alone. To paraphrase the American journalist Frank Manovich, it is not factual, but it is accurate. Gonzo journalism shows that or at least asks are not the particular subjective filters by which facts and events are processed and imagined in a moment in history as relevant as the facts themselves in understanding the truth of that moment or at least a slice of the truth. Throughout the decades following Fear and Loathing in Las Vegas, Thompson would continue to write a variety of pieces in a variety of forms. Most notably, he wrote and published Fear and Loathing on the campaign trail in 1973, which using Gonzo style recounts Thompson's experience following the 1972 presidential election, all while satarizing and critiquing political journalism itself, showcasing the malleability and biases inbuilt in the coverage of politicians. This book too was a huge success. By this point, in the mid70s, Thompson's public persona was beginning to outpace him. He became a rockstar journalist known by nearly everyone and adored by Hollywood's biggest names. He soon had multiple movies based on him and his work. Carrying out journalistic endeavors became increasingly difficult for him and his vision of writing began to blur, further induced by heavy substance and self-abuse. As the lifelong companions of drugs and chaotic behavior nestled closer, the lines between his larger than-l life character in his work, his public persona, and his true self began to blur. I'm really in the way as a person and the myth has taken over. Thompson said, "I find myself in a pendage. I'm not only no longer necessary, I'm in the way. It'd be much better if I died. Then people could take the myth and make films." Though Thompson's persona is deeply intertwined and pertinent to a story, it can often overshadow a much more subtle, humble, compassionate, and remarkably articulate essence that deserves equal spotlight. He was not merely the character he portrayed in his work and public life, but the man who cared enough and was talented enough to create this character in order to explore, understand, and represent a very nuanced condition of the world during his time. In the end, Thompson sort of found his American dream, not necessarily in the romantic sense, but in the holistic sense, the sense that includes that form found in Las Vegas. He lived with a deep, rebellious spirit, sure to always go his own way. Going right up to the edges of every cliff he reached, looking out at the vast plains of past, present, and future, staring down the lethal drop offs, but a few inches from his toes. Not without fear, but in spite of it. It is often said that there's a fine line between genius and crazy. And it can perhaps be said that Hunter S. Thompson snorted that line. He lived with both the chaos, courage, and depravity that he wrote about. He indulged in the excess. He was a product of the American dream and thus an embodiment of it. And if his story, his American dream, couldn't be any more poetically American, it ended with a gun. The American dream is perhaps alive and well, it just might be lethal. Thompson's life and work served as both a welcoming of a new style of journalism and a warning against the state of the Western world, America in particular. His work questions where all our dreaming goes, what happens when we awake the next morning? It critiques the luxury of excess and asks, "Is it all worth it what we are chasing?" But perhaps most importantly, it answers the question that Thompson's friend asked him when he was still just 22 years old. In that affforementioned letter about what one ought to do in life, Thompson continued, "Every man is the sum total of his reactions to experience. As your experiences differ and multiply, you become a different man and hence your perspective changes. The answer then must not deal with goals at all or not with tangible goals anyway. In short, a man who has found meaning has not dedicated his life to reaching a predefined goal, but he has rather chosen a way of life he knows he will enjoy. The goal is absolutely secondary. It is the functioning toward the goal which is important. The directions we go in life are far less important than whose finger it is that we are following. In Thompson's case, that trembling pointing finger always remained his own, even up until the last choice he made. And in this, he reminds us in the most extreme sense to take control over our lives and live them on our own terms, to step outside the lines, to curve and blur them, to make new ones, to inhale them. He shows us that our work and our lives are always an ongoing process of change, of effort, of labor, of pain, of hope, of laughing, of tripping, of meandering, of loving, and of dying in hopes that the story will continue in the words of others. His life was as much a part of his work as the words on the pages. Each breath as much as each line, revealing the difficulties of making it through this world with any resemblance to the person you were when you came in, while also showing the unbelievable thrill of the whole [Music] process. In order to better find the directions we want to go in life, in order to better follow through with those directions, adopting the nuances of existence along the way, we must continually be learning and considering the lessons and insights of others from those who have already gone certain routes, thinkers, experts, and doers. vehicle. Far more unsettling, there appears to   be no obvious consensus about its direction. Where 
is it going? Where does it end? Does it end? Will   it be good for us? Do we even have a say?
In 2008, Benjamin Noys, professor of   critical theory at the University of 
Chichester, wrote a blogpost. In it,   he defined a term that would go on to refer 
to an incredibly dark, strange, and complex   political philosophy that has become increasingly 
popular throughout the 2010s and 2020s. The term   was accelerationism (which was inspired 
by the fictional group in Zelazny’s novel,   Lord of Light). Fundamentally, accelerationism is 
defined as the belief that increasing the speed   of capitalistic and technological advancement and 
intensifying the problems that come as a result   to the point of some form of societal collapse 
or significant transfiguration is a necessary   and good thing. For an accelerationist, the sort 
of dizzying, uncontrollable speed of technology   and societal challenges, of which we are 
increasingly experiencing in the modern world,   should be leaned into and further accelerated. 
The current system is doomed to fail, so we should   simply cause it to fail sooner. Why we should 
do this and why this is a good thing largely   depends on who you ask and which variant of 
accelerationism the person you ask subscribes to.  The origins of accelerationism are widely 
attributed to the English philosopher   and professor, Nick Land. In 1995, at the 
University of Warwick, Land along with several   other individuals formed what become known as the 
Cybernetic Culture Research Unit, or the CCRU. The   CCRU was an experimental and rebel academic group 
with one of its main goals being, according to one   of its key members, Sadie Plant, “to undermine 
the cheery utopianism of the 90s … We wanted a   more open, convoluted, complicated world, 
not a shiny new order.” And on that goal,   they largely delivered. Land and the other members 
of the CCRU weaved together ideas from philosophy,   science-fiction, cybernetics, and the occult to 
create theories and ideas surrounding culture,   technology, psychology, reality, and, most 
importantly, how these domains interact in   subversive feedback loops. Overtime, the CCRU 
would devolve more and more into occult-like   and even satanic-like tendencies, which can 
likely be attributed to, at least in part,   the heavy substance use and mental breakdowns 
amongst members—particularly Land. Soon,   the group, as an organized unit, would dwindle 
to an end. But the ideas it developed and   disseminated would survive and go on to evolve 
into and inspire various forms of accelerationism.  Accelerationism is unique for several reasons. 
One particularly notable one is that it can be   found on both the extreme right and extreme left 
of the political spectrum. Two worldviews that   couldn’t be much further apart from one another 
can seemingly meet, with some level of agreement,   on this idea. Of course, as one might imagine, 
there are substantial differences as to why these   opposing groups embrace the philosophy.
The version of accelerationism typically   associated with the left, which was largely 
inspired by the CCRU member Mark Fisher,   is the view that accelerating technology and 
capitalism to near or complete breaking points is   a good thing because there are better alternative 
versions of the world that are only possible on   the other side of this world’s collapse. The term 
collapse here does not necessarily need to mean a   total, apocalyptic-like destruction—though 
for some individuals, it might—but rather,   an end to the current capitalist world as we know 
it, resulting in a post-capitalist paradigm. Some   individuals on this side of the philosophy 
hold the belief that technologies like AI and   automation will ultimately take tedious work off 
the human plate, expand equality and freedom for   all, and increase the overall quality of the 
human experience. Technology, in this view,   is essentially the capitalist vehicle needed to 
reach a post-capitalist society. We are on a long,   hard road but we are heading for a favorable 
destination. We should therefore accelerate toward   it, even if this means we might violently 
sideswipe some things along the way. The   sooner we arrive, the sooner things get better.
Individuals on the right side of the philosophy,   like Land, don’t believe in this post-capitalist 
utopian vision. For them, things are much,   much bleaker. “The notion that self-propelling 
technology is separable from capitalism is a   deep theoretical error,” said Land. Instead, 
these individuals believe that through the   rapid development of capitalistic and 
technological forces, the entire global   system will overcome its limitations and achieve 
its full potential, which will ultimately result   in a technological consumption (or destruction) 
of humanity and perhaps the world as a whole—a   dystopian-looking scenario with transhumanist 
or post-humanist rulers, massive inequality,   and corporatized control. This all, however, 
is important and good to the natural order   and development of existence. At the very least, 
it is inevitable. For some of these individuals,   the point of accelerating it all is more or less 
to just get the whole thing over with. That is,   to cause the inevitable self-annihilating outcome 
to occur as soon as possible. The vehicle we are   in is not headed for a favorable destination, but 
rather, a wall. We are traveling at fatal speeds,   and we cannot stop, get out, or change directions. 
We can see the wall, and so, by accelerating,   we will cause the crash to happen quicker and 
spare us of the dread and chaos that would   otherwise form, persist, and increase in the 
anticipation of the crash. For some, perhaps   anticipation of pain is worse than pain itself.
For all functional purposes, accelerationism is   extremely obscure with no meaningful, singular 
vision for what the world will be, should be,   or why. The entire concept is somewhere 
between philosophy, gospel, science-fiction,   occult theory, and a potentially self-fulling 
doomsday prophecy. But perhaps what’s equally if   not more interesting is what accelerationism 
seems to point to about humanity’s current   state and sensibilities in general.
In many ways, at this point in history,   it’s much easier to imagine a world without 
humanity at all than to imagine a world that   isn’t fully intertwined with capitalism 
and technological advancement. “[There is   a] widespread sense that not only is capitalism 
the only viable political and economic system,   but also that it is now impossible even to imagine 
a coherent alternative to it,” wrote Mark Fisher.   We are seemingly locked in this technocaptalistic 
vehicle, destined to go wherever it goes. But   that’s just it. Where is it going? And why is it 
going? There appears to be no one in the driver’s   seat. There is no clear navigation system or 
central orientation. There is just this ceaseless,   aimless, and inescapable forward motion.
A popular sentiment amongst many powerful   modern individuals, like tech-company founders, 
CEOs, and investors as well as technocrats   and futurists more broadly, is the belief that 
progress is ultimately a natural and good thing,   but it is not really something anyone can control. 
According to the mathematical physicist, quantum   computing researcher, and tech company founder 
Guillaume Verdon, “The goal is for the human   technocapital mimetic machine to become self-aware 
and to hyperstiously engineer its own growth … To   lean into the natural tendencies of the system 
to adapt for its own growth.” If this is true,   this raises the following questions: If it is not 
us controlling it, what is? What is determining   its hypersitions, success, direction, and growth? 
Is it truly purely in line with the metaphysical   and natural order of the world? How do we 
know, and how do we know this is a good thing?  With this line of thinking increasingly gaining 
popularity and control over the modern world,   technology is becoming our new God. It is 
all knowing, all powerful, and all good.   It is the Abrahamic God that must be worshiped 
despite consequence or reason. It will create   heaven and hell; it will know the wickedness and 
goodness of the world; it will cause the floods;   and it will usher in the apocalypse to make way 
for new order and reorientation. But all will be   good, all will be part of its divine plan.
Of course, there are very good, rational   reasons to welcome some degree of significant 
technological and capitalistic advancement.   In many cases, the results of new technologies 
can be incredible, fully deserving of the awe   and reverence it receives. Technologies have and 
will continue to save and improve the lives of   many people—in health and medicine, connectivity, 
communication, accessibility, creative expression,   safety, and so on. But the problem remains, 
particularly with the scope and scale in the   modern world, technology cannot be narrowly 
controlled or focused onto any particular area   or application. An AI that can easily diagnose 
ailments and prescribe medical treatments can also   easily diagnose an individual’s psychology and 
execute effective manipulation strategies. AI and   automation that can easily execute real time stock 
trading, assist with artistic and entrepreneurial   creation, or carry out shipping and fulfillment 
tasks, can also be used in warfare, create and   distribute art and services indiscernible from 
what is human-made, or maliciously alter economic   and political systems. Technology is not a 
hose that is used to put specific fires out;   it’s always an opening of a floodgate. It will put 
out fires, but it will also destroy many things.  The truth is, no one knows if something terrible 
or great is going to happen—or how terrible or   how great the things that will happen will be. But 
our goal, obviously, should not be to accelerate   bad things or knowingly permit bad things to 
happen—wherever possible. We should not hinge   our hopes on the romantic ideal that haphazardly 
causing or allowing things to go how they are will   ultimately lead to a net good. Part of what makes 
humanity as powerful as it is, is the ability to   consider what is happening, to self-reflect, and 
to self-correct. Perhaps we are not as helpless   in the vehicle of technology and capitalism as we 
might think. Perhaps we can apply the brakes in   certain moments. Perhaps we can steer in different 
directions on occasion. And perhaps we can consult   each other in increasingly meaningful, effective 
ways to develop a more synergetic orientation and   navigation system. Of course, this is all much, 
much easier said than done, and perhaps, on some   level, it is naïve. But if we have the power to 
create what we create, why not also the power   to wield what we create in more careful ways?
Our hope for our relationship and future with   technology, like all things, starts with a better 
understanding and relationship with ourselves.   We must deeply recognize our fallibility and 
ignorance—even with, and perhaps especially with,   the augmentation of technology. Technology 
merely inherits our fallibility and distributes   it at scale. We must move through this world 
with a humility, thoughtfulness, and care,   recognizing that we often don’t know what the 
world will be like or should be like; that we   often don’t know what the world needs or what will 
make it a so-called better place. We don’t yet   even properly know what we need as individuals. 
Perhaps we should strive, as individuals and as   a humanity, at least at times, to slow down—in our 
assessments and aspirations—to look in and down,   instead of always up and forward. Our problems 
are not merely technological but psychological. regardless of what direction the future goes

us believe that open-mindedness and tolerance   are good things. Most of us believe that we are 
open-minded and tolerant. Throughout our lives,   we are repeatedly told in different contexts to do 
and see things in an open-minded way. As a child,   we were likely told to be open-minded about trying 
new foods and going to new places. As a teenager,   we were likely told to be open-minded (at least 
in so many words) about doing something new or   outside our comfort zone. And throughout our whole 
life, we have likely been told to be open-minded   about different kinds of people, cultures, 
opinions, and ideologies that perhaps we aren’t   accustomed to or don’t personally like. Of course, 
in many cases, open-mindedness and tolerance are   in fact very good qualities that are crucial 
to so many things—learning, growing, loving,   sympathizing, accepting, and coexisting. But far 
less considered is the fact that open-mindedness   and tolerance can also be terrible things. 
Consider the following thought experiment.  ***
You and a few other people are forming   an organization in your community. Its title is 
The Tolerance and Antidiscrimination Group. Its   goal is to fight discrimination and intolerance 
through a variety of initiatives—like discussions,   education, policy, fundraising, and so on. 
The response is fantastic. A huge number   of people want to join. In fact, at least one 
or more individuals from every belief system,   worldview, gender, race, sexuality, 
national origin, age, and so on sign up.   Things appear to be off to a great start.
At the first meeting, however, there’s a   problem—a big one. Some individuals don’t like 
other members. They don’t know any of them,   but they don’t like them based on their beliefs, 
personal preferences, or who they are. These   prejudiced members quickly become vocal about 
their disdain, making terrible comments about the   other individuals. Panicked and unnerved by the 
situation, you quickly separate all the prejudiced   individuals and inform them that they must leave.
“So, we’re being discriminated against because of   our beliefs?” one of the individuals says to you 
with a frustrated, matter-of-fact tone. “I thought   this was the antidiscrimination and tolerance 
group where everyone is welcome. How you can   you not include everyone if your mission is 
the inclusion and tolerance of everyone?”  This confuses you. Everything this individual is 
saying is true, but of course, something seems   very wrong. Unsure of exactly what to do and 
wanting to honor the group’s mission totally,   you agree to let all the individuals stay.
Over the following few weeks, you and the group   make efforts to improve cohesion, understanding, 
treatment, and opportunity for all within the   community—and hopefully beyond. The problems of 
incoherence, misunderstanding, and mistreatment,   however, are running rampant within the 
organization itself. Many individuals have   even left, having felt increasingly uncomfortable 
with the way they were being perceived and treated   by the prejudiced members. On several occasions, 
some of the prejudiced individuals implied,   and in some cases even explicitly stated, that 
those who conflicted with their own beliefs and   partialities should leave. The group, which 
started out as an inclusive organization   whose goal was tolerance and acceptance, 
is now becoming exclusive and intolerant   to many. Realizing something needs to change, 
you confront the problem individuals again.  “Sorry, but you guys have to leave. 
You’re creating a huge problem for   our other members,” you tell them.
“So, we’re being discriminated against   because of our beliefs and views? I thought 
this was the antidiscrimination and tolerance   group,” they say to you again.
This time, you’ve prepared   yourself for this objection.
“Yes, but your beliefs and actions   are harming others. The point of tolerance 
and open-mindedness is to allow for cohesion   and acceptance, not harm and ostracization.”
“But many of the beliefs and attitudes and   behaviors of the other individuals’ who 
were and are in the group make us feel   threatened and uncomfortable,” one person 
says earnestly. “We don’t believe what we   believe to be hateful. We just believe it to be

and tolerance group descends further and further   into a smaller group comprising mostly intolerant 
and bigoted individuals. Worse, some of these   individuals begin to threaten and even use
